--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 1:**

The champion is a 1-state GoAwayColor from GREEN (clr=2) machine with score -11.93. This is remarkable - robots flee green walls, which means they avoid the GREEN half of the source and naturally drift toward the BLUE half or nest area. Wait, let me reconsider: clr=2 is GREEN. GoAwayColor from GREEN means robots move away from green objects. The green walls are behind one half of the source. So robots flee green → they go toward the nest (red wall side) or blue side.

Actually, the key insight: GoAwayColor from GREEN (clr=2) gets score -11.93. This means robots are delivering beneficial items. They flee green walls → they end up near the nest or blue source area. If the blue half decreases score by 1 per item, robots going to blue side and then delivering to nest would score well.

But wait - a 1-state machine with no transitions means robots just constantly flee green. They'd go to the nest side (away from green). If they happen to pick up blue items when crossing the source, then deliver them to the nest... that could work.

**Strategy:**
1. Keep the champion (survival)
2. Exploit: variations of GoAwayColor green with additional states for navigation
3. Explore: GoToColor blue (clr=3) + delivery to nest (GoToColor white/red), and other combinations

Let me think about what colors map to what:
- clr=1: ? clr=2: GREEN clr=3: BLUE clr=4: YELLOW clr=5: MAGENTA clr=6: CYAN
- Actually standard: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

The nest wall is RED (clr=1), source has BLUE (clr=3) and GREEN (clr=2) walls.

Good foraging: go to BLUE source (pick up blue item), then go to nest (go toward RED wall or white floor).

The champion flees green → naturally goes toward blue or nest. This works passively.

**14 machines:**
1. Champion (exact copy)
2. GoAwayColor green, 1-state, different LED
3. 2-state: GoToColor blue (s0), when on black floor → GoToColor red/nest (s1), when on white floor → back to s0
4. 2-state: GoAwayColor green (s0), on white floor (nest) stay/explore (s1)
5. 2-state: GoToColor blue (s0) → on black→ GoAwayColor green toward nest (s1) → on white back
6. 3-state with blue seeking, nest delivery, exploration
7. GoToColor blue only (1-state)
8. 2-state: exploration + GoAwayColor green
9. 3-state refined foraging loop
10. GoAwayColor green + when on white explore
11. 2-state GoAwayColor green → on white stop briefly
12. 3-state with attraction + GoAwayColor green
13. GoToColor red (toward nest) 1-state
14. 2-state GoToColor blue + GoToColor red

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 2:**

The clear pattern: GoAwayColor GREEN (clr=2) dominates. The top 3 machines all score around -11.4, and they're essentially the same 1-state GoAwayColor GREEN machine. The champion stays at -11.93.

The 2-state machine ranked 3rd (score -11.43) has both states doing GoAwayColor GREEN - essentially the same behavior, confirming GoAwayColor GREEN is the core strategy.

Machines that add GoToColor BLUE (clr=3) actually perform worse (scores -9.44 to -10.07 range, or even positive scores). The GoToColor BLUE approach leads robots to the source but picks up wrong items.

**Why does GoAwayColor GREEN work?**
- Robots flee green walls → they move toward the nest side
- They pass through the black source area (picking up items) en route
- They deliver to the white nest area
- The blue half decreases score, so blue items are good

**Strategy for Round 3:**
- Keep champion
- Exploit: more variations of GoAwayColor GREEN, possibly with smarter transitions
- Try: GoAwayColor GREEN as primary, with transition on WHITE floor (nest detected) → brief exploration or stop, then back
- Try adding a transition to GoToColor BLUE when not seeing green (to seek source)
- Try GoAwayColor GREEN with neighbor-based transitions to spread robots

The 2-state machine ranked 4th (-11.20) shows GoAwayColor GREEN → on white floor → Exploration works reasonably. Let me refine this.

Key: avoid GoToColor BLUE (clr=3) as primary state - it hurts performance.

Note: LED colors must be 0, 4, 5, 6 only (no 3!).

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 3:**

The champion is now a 2-state machine: GoAwayColor GREEN → on WHITE floor (nest) → Exploration with rwm=20, then on BLACK → back to GoAwayColor GREEN, or on GRAY → back too. Score -11.93.

The pattern is very clear:
- **State 0**: GoAwayColor GREEN (clr=2) - flee green walls, moving toward nest
- **Transition**: on WHITE floor (p=0.85) → go to State 1
- **State 1**: Exploration (rwm=20) - random walk briefly
- **Transitions**: on BLACK (p=0.8) → back to state 0, on GRAY (p=0.9) → back to state 0

This makes sense: robots flee green → reach nest (white floor) → explore briefly → find black source area → pick up blue items → flee green again → deliver to nest.

**What to try:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Fine-tune the champion's parameters (p=0.85 transition, rwm=20, p=0.8/0.9 back transitions)
3. Try higher/lower rwm values (10, 15, 25, 30)
4. Try different white-floor probabilities (0.75, 0.80, 0.90, 0.95)
5. Try adding a 3rd state for more nuanced behavior
6. Try different back-transition probabilities

The machine ranked 14th (score 0.42) had p=0.92 on white+p=0.7 on gray → this failed badly, suggesting the gray transition matters. The champion uses p=0.9 on gray which is key.

The machine ranked 12th (-9.47) transitioned on BLACK (p=0.7) to exploration instead of WHITE - this is worse.

**Fine-tuning focus:**
- White transition: try 0.80, 0.82, 0.87, 0.90
- rwm: try 10, 15, 18, 22, 25
- Back transitions: keep p~0.8 for black, p~0.9 for gray

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 4:**

The champion is now: GoAwayColor GREEN → on WHITE (p=0.85) → Exploration rwm=20 → on BLACK (p=0.85) back, on GRAY (p=0.85) back. Score -12.10.

The critical difference from previous champion: both back-transitions use p=0.85 (symmetric), rather than 0.8/0.9. This is a subtle but important tweak.

**What's working:**
- The 2-state topology: GoAwayColor GREEN (s0) + Exploration (s1) is consistently best
- White-floor trigger at p=0.85 is optimal
- rwm=20 seems best (15 and 25 are slightly worse)
- Symmetric back-transitions (0.85/0.85) beat asymmetric (0.8/0.9)

**What to try for Round 5:**
- Fine-tune: p=0.83, 0.84, 0.86, 0.87 for back-transitions
- Fine-tune: rwm=18, 19, 21, 22
- Fine-tune: white trigger at 0.83, 0.84, 0.86, 0.87
- Try adding a 3rd transition from state 1 (white floor back to state 0 too)
- Try 3-state with a brief "stop" in nest
- The ranked 2nd machine had 3 transitions from s1 including white (0.6) - interesting

The big insight: the machine needs robots to cycle: flee green → reach nest (white) → explore → find black source → back to flee green. The p=0.85 symmetric back-transitions keep the cycle going efficiently.

**Avoid:** 
- GoToColor BLUE (positive scores)
- rwm too high (50) or too low
- Asymmetric back-transitions with extreme values

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 5:**

The new champion is a 3-state machine (-12.32):
- s0: GoAwayColor GREEN → on WHITE (p=0.85) → go to s1
- s1: Exploration rwm=20 → on BLACK (p=0.85) → back to s0; on GRAY (p=0.85) → go to s2
- s2: GoAwayColor GREEN → on GRAY (p=0.85) → back to s1; on BLACK (p=0.5) → back to s1

So state 2 is a secondary GoAwayColor GREEN that routes back through exploration. This adds nuance: when on gray after exploring, robots go to a second flee-green state, then return to exploration.

The 2nd best (-12.02) adds a WHITE floor transition from s1 back to s0 (p=0.5) - interesting.

**What to explore for Round 6:**

1. Keep champion exactly
2. Variations of the 3-state topology:
   - Tweak s2's transition probabilities
   - Try different rwm values in s1
   - Try p=0.80, 0.90 on white trigger
3. The s2 gray→s1 (p=0.85) and black→s1 (p=0.5) asymmetry is interesting - try other values
4. 4-state extension: add a 4th state
5. Try the 2nd best with modifications (3-transition s1 with white return)

Key insight: s2 acts as a "second pass" flee-green to help robots that are on gray get back toward the source efficiently.

Let me focus on:
- Fine-tuning s2's transitions (p=0.5 for black seems low, try 0.6, 0.7)
- Fine-tuning s2's gray transition (try 0.80, 0.90)
- Try 4-state extensions
- Keep the successful 2-state variants

## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 6:**

The new champion is a 4-state machine (-12.50):
- s0: GoAwayColor GREEN → on WHITE (p=0.85) → s1
- s1: Exploration rwm=20 → on BLACK (p=0.85) → s0; on GRAY (p=0.85) → s2
- s2: GoAwayColor GREEN → on GRAY (p=0.85) → s2 (self-loop!); on BLACK (p=0.5) → s1
- s3: Exploration rwm=20 → on BLACK (p=0.85) → s0; on GRAY (p=0.85) → s0

Wait, let me re-read: `--n2x0 2` means destination index 2 among other states. With 4 states, leaving s2, others are [0,1,3]: D=0→s0, D=1→s1, D=2→s3. So s2→s3 on gray, s2→s1 on black.

s3: Exploration → on BLACK→s0, on GRAY→s0.

So the flow is: flee green → nest(white) → explore(s1) → gray → flee green(s2) → gray → explore more(s3) → back to start.

**Key structural insight:** The 4-state machine adds a second exploration state (s3) that both transitions go back to s0. This creates a longer cycle with more exploration before returning to flee-green.

**What to try for Round 7:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Tweak s2's black→s1 probability (try 0.4, 0.6, 0.7)
3. Tweak s3's back transitions (try p=0.80, 0.90)
4. Try different rwm values for s3 (15, 25, 30)
5. Try s3 as GoAwayColor GREEN instead of Exploration
6. Try variations where s3 transitions go to s1 instead of s0
7. The 13th-ranked machine (-11.19) shows that gray→gray (C=1) in s2 hurts - avoid that

Note: In the champion, s2 has `--n2x0 2` (destination index 2 among others = s3) and `--n2x1 1` (destination index 1 = s1). S3 has `--n3x0 0` (= s0) and `--n3x1 0` (= s0) - both go to s0.

## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 8:**

The champion (-12.50) still holds from Round 6. None of the round 8 machines beat it. The best new machines scored -12.28, which is close but not better.

Interesting findings:
- rwm=12 and rwm=30 both match rwm=20 at -12.28 (all equivalent within noise)
- Flipping s1's transition destinations (black→s2, gray→s0) scores -12.26 (close)
- Adding white-floor transition from s1 scores -12.26 (close)
- s3 going to s2 on black instead of s0 scores -12.26 (close)
- Changing p0x0 to 0.88 hurts (-11.56), 0.82 hurts slightly (-12.10)
- s3=GoAwayColor GREEN hurts (-11.62)
- Changing s2's black trigger from C=0 to C=2 (white) hurts (-11.46)

The champion is quite robust. I'm near a local optimum. Let me try:

1. **Keep champion** (mandatory)
2. **Different exploration strategies**: Try s3 with more random walk (rwm=5, 8, 50, 100)
3. **Modify s2 behavior**: Try adding a third transition from s2 (e.g., on WHITE→s0)
4. **Try varying the p=0.5** in s2's black transition more carefully (0.3, 0.35, 0.45, 0.55, 0.6)
5. **Exploration topology**: Try having s3 transition to s2 on gray (instead of s0)
6. **Try completely different s3**: Stop behavior, or Repulsion
7. **Vary p=0.85 more carefully on s2's gray transition**: 0.80, 0.82, 0.87, 0.90

The scores are clustering around -12.1 to -12.3, suggesting we're near a plateau. Let me try some genuinely different modifications while keeping the core structure.

Key: the champion's unique feature is `--n2x0 2` (s2's gray transition goes to s3, not s1). Most variants I tried with `--n2x0 0` (→s0) or `--n2x0 1` (→s1) seem to score around -12.12. The s3 exploration seems key.

Let me try:
- Different s2 black transition probabilities: 0.35, 0.45, 0.55
- s3 with different rwm: 5, 8, 10, 50
- s3 also having a white-floor escape
- Adding 3rd transition to s2

# PROMPT SYSTEME

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
